+<span class="a">+into a dwelling.  In determining whether a product is a consumer product,</span>
+<span class="a">+doubtful cases shall be resolved in favor of coverage.  For a particular</span>
+<span class="a">+product received by a particular user, &quot;normally used&quot; refers to a</span>
+<span class="a">+typical or common use of that class of product, regardless of the status</span>
+<span class="a">+of the particular user or of the way in which the particular user</span>
+<span class="a">+actually uses, or expects or is expected to use, the product.  A product</span>
+<span class="a">+is a consumer product regardless of whether the product has substantial</span>
+<span class="a">+commercial, industrial or non-consumer uses, unless such uses represent</span>
+<span class="a">+the only significant mode of use of the product.</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>

+<span class="a">+and execute modified versions of a covered work in that User Product from</span>

+diff --git a/README.md b/README.md
+new file mode 100644
+index 0000000..2fd1c77
+--- /dev/null
++++ b/README.md
+<span class="h">@@ -0,0 +1,34 @@</span>
+<span class="a">+# CSV</span>
+<span class="a">+Defines a lisp reader to read is CSV:</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+`#!field,field,field!#`</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+which is used to read in CSV files with `slurp`:</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+`(slurp #p&quot;example.csv&quot;)`</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+and the result is a list:</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+``` common-lisp</span>
+<span class="a">+CL-USER&gt; (csv:slurp #p&quot;example.csv&quot;)</span>
+<span class="a">+(&quot;example&quot; &quot;csv&quot; &quot;list&quot; &quot;result&quot; &quot;like&quot; &quot; this&quot;)</span>
+<span class="a">+```</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+This program invokes the lisp reader, making it:</span>
+<span class="a">+1) Potentially risky to use if the input it not trusted.</span>
+<span class="a">+2) Resource intensive for medium to large files (&gt;5MB).</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+use with care.</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+# Features:</span>
+<span class="a">+Supports escaping quotes the CSV way, with quad quotes:</span>
+<span class="a">+csv   -&gt; lisp</span>
+<span class="a">+`&quot;&quot;&quot;&quot;`-&gt;`&quot;\&quot;&quot;`</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+# Installation</span>
+<span class="a">+Use ASDF:</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+``` common-lisp</span>
+<span class="a">+CL-USER&gt; (asdf:load-system :csv)</span>
+<span class="a">+T</span>
+<span class="a">+```</span>
+diff --git a/csv.asd b/csv.asd
+new file mode 100644
+index 0000000..35b1887
+--- /dev/null
++++ b/csv.asd
+<span class="h">@@ -0,0 +1,9 @@</span>
+<span class="a">+(asdf:defsystem :csv</span>
+<span class="a">+  :version &quot;0.1.0&quot;</span>
+<span class="a">+  :description  &quot;Read CSV files into lists.&quot;</span>
+<span class="a">+  :author       &quot;Spenser Truex spensertruex.com&quot;</span>
+<span class="a">+  :serial       t</span>
+<span class="a">+  :license      &quot;GNU GPL, version 3&quot;</span>
+<span class="a">+  :components   ((:file &quot;package&quot;)</span>
+<span class="a">+                 (:file &quot;utils&quot;)</span>
+<span class="a">+                 (:file &quot;csv&quot;)))</span>
+diff --git a/csv.lisp b/csv.lisp
+new file mode 100644
+index 0000000..6d5bbb0
+--- /dev/null
++++ b/csv.lisp
+<span class="h">@@ -0,0 +1,55 @@</span>
+<span class="a">+(in-package :csv)</span>
+<span class="a">+(defun make-escapep (prev curr)</span>
+<span class="a">+  (char= #\&quot; prev curr))</span>
+<span class="a">+(defun end-tokenp (prev curr)</span>
+<span class="a">+  (declare (ignore curr))</span>
+<span class="a">+  (char= #\&quot; prev))</span>
+<span class="a">+(defun openp (prev curr)</span>
+<span class="a">+  (declare (ignore curr))</span>
+<span class="a">+  (char= #\&quot; prev))</span>
+<span class="a">+(let ((in nil)</span>
+<span class="a">+      (escape nil))</span>
+<span class="a">+  (setf (fdefinition &#x27;switches)</span>
+<span class="a">+	(lambda (prev curr str)</span>
+<span class="a">+	  (let ((str (if (array-has-fill-pointer-p str)</span>
+<span class="a">+                         str</span>
+<span class="a">+                         (make-array 1 :fill-pointer 0</span>
+<span class="a">+                                       :adjustable t</span>
+<span class="a">+                                       :element-type &#x27;character))))</span>
+<span class="a">+            (if (null curr)</span>
+<span class="a">+	        (progn (setf in nil escape nil) str)</span>
+<span class="a">+	        (if in</span>
+<span class="a">+		    (if escape</span>
+<span class="a">+		        (progn (toggle escape) (push-on prev str))</span>
+<span class="a">+		        (if (make-escapep prev curr)</span>
+<span class="a">+			    (progn (toggle escape) str)</span>
+<span class="a">+			    (if (end-tokenp prev curr)</span>
+<span class="a">+			        (progn (toggle in) (push-on #\Nul str))</span>
+<span class="a">+			        (push-on prev str))))</span>
+<span class="a">+		    (if (openp prev curr)</span>
+<span class="a">+		        (progn (toggle in) str)</span>
+<span class="a">+		        str)))))))</span>
+<span class="a">+(defun splice (str)</span>
+<span class="a">+  (labels ((inner (str acc!)</span>
+<span class="a">+             (if (string= &quot;&quot; str)</span>
+<span class="a">+                 (nreverse acc!)</span>
+<span class="a">+                 (let ((res (search (string #\Nul) str)))</span>
+<span class="a">+                   (inner (subseq str (1+ res)) (push (subseq str 0 res) acc!))))))</span>
+<span class="a">+    (inner str nil)))</span>
+<span class="a">+(make-reader my-csv (stream #\# #\!)</span>
+<span class="a">+  (macrolet ((once (&amp;body body) ``&#x27;,,@body))</span>
+<span class="a">+    (do ((prev #1= (read-char stream nil nil) curr)</span>
+<span class="a">+	 (curr #1# #1#)</span>
+<span class="a">+	 (str (make-array 0 :adjustable t :element-type &#x27;character)</span>
+<span class="a">+	      (switches prev curr str)))</span>
+<span class="a">+	((and (char= curr #\#)</span>
+<span class="a">+              (char= prev #\!)) (once (splice (switches nil nil str)))))))</span>
+<span class="a">+(defun read-to-string (stream)</span>
+<span class="a">+  (with-output-to-string (s)</span>
+<span class="a">+    (dolines (line stream)</span>
+<span class="a">+      (princ line s)</span>
+<span class="a">+      (format s &quot;~%&quot;))))</span>
+<span class="a">+(defun slurp (path)</span>
+<span class="a">+  &quot;Read in a CSV file. Resource intensive, invokes the lisp reader.&quot;</span>
+<span class="a">+  (with-open-file (s path)</span>
+<span class="a">+    (concatenate &#x27;string &quot;#!&quot; (read-to-string s) &quot;!#&quot;)))</span>
+diff --git a/package.lisp b/package.lisp
+new file mode 100644
+index 0000000..c0b5a5c
+--- /dev/null
++++ b/package.lisp
+<span class="h">@@ -0,0 +1,4 @@</span>
+<span class="a">+(in-package :cl-user)</span>
+<span class="a">+(defpackage #:csv</span>
+<span class="a">+  (:use #:cl)</span>
+<span class="a">+  (:export :slurp))</span>
+diff --git a/utils.lisp b/utils.lisp
+new file mode 100644
+index 0000000..e26831a
+--- /dev/null
++++ b/utils.lisp
+<span class="h">@@ -0,0 +1,20 @@</span>
+<span class="a">+(in-package :csv)</span>
+<span class="a">+(defmacro dolines ((var stream) &amp;body body)</span>
+<span class="a">+  `(do ((,var #1=(read-line ,stream nil nil) #1#))</span>
+<span class="a">+       ((null ,var))</span>
+<span class="a">+     ,@body))</span>
+<span class="a">+(defmacro with-gensyms (symbols &amp;body body)</span>
+<span class="a">+  &quot;Create gensyms for those symbols.&quot;</span>
+<span class="a">+  `(let (,@(mapcar #&#x27;(lambda (sym)</span>
+<span class="a">+		       `(,sym &#x27;,(gensym))) symbols))</span>
+<span class="a">+     ,@body))</span>
+<span class="a">+(defmacro make-reader (name (stream-var dispatch-1 dispatch-2) &amp;body body)</span>
+<span class="a">+  &quot;Simplify the reader macro creation process in most cases.&quot;</span>
+<span class="a">+  (with-gensyms (sub-char numarg)</span>
+<span class="a">+    `(progn (defun ,name (,stream-var ,sub-char ,numarg)</span>
+<span class="a">+	      (declare (ignore ,sub-char ,numarg))</span>
+<span class="a">+	      ,@body)</span>
+<span class="a">+	    (set-dispatch-macro-character ,dispatch-1 ,dispatch-2 #&#x27;,name))))</span>
+<span class="a">+(defun push-on (elt stack)</span>
+<span class="a">+  (vector-push-extend elt stack) stack)</span>
+<span class="a">+(define-modify-macro toggle () not &quot;Flipping vars.&quot;)</span></pre>
+</div></body></html>

+<a id="l17" href="#l17">17</a> software for all its users.  We, the Free Software Foundation, use the
+<a id="l18" href="#l18">18</a> GNU General Public License for most of our software; it applies also to
+<a id="l19" href="#l19">19</a> any other work released this way by its authors.  You can apply it to
+<a id="l20" href="#l20">20</a> your programs, too.
+<a id="l21" href="#l21">21</a> 
+<a id="l22" href="#l22">22</a>   When we speak of free software, we are referring to freedom, not
+<a id="l23" href="#l23">23</a> price.  Our General Public Licenses are designed to make sure that you
+<a id="l24" href="#l24">24</a> have the freedom to distribute copies of free software (and charge for
+<a id="l25" href="#l25">25</a> them if you wish), that you receive source code or can get it if you
+<a id="l26" href="#l26">26</a> want it, that you can change the software or use pieces of it in new
+<a id="l27" href="#l27">27</a> free programs, and that you know you can do these things.
+<a id="l28" href="#l28">28</a> 
+<a id="l29" href="#l29">29</a>   To protect your rights, we need to prevent others from denying you
+<a id="l30" href="#l30">30</a> these rights or asking you to surrender the rights.  Therefore, you have
+<a id="l31" href="#l31">31</a> certain responsibilities if you distribute copies of the software, or if
+<a id="l32" href="#l32">32</a> you modify it: responsibilities to respect the freedom of others.
+<a id="l33" href="#l33">33</a> 
+<a id="l34" href="#l34">34</a>   For example, if you distribute copies of such a program, whether
+<a id="l35" href="#l35">35</a> gratis or for a fee, you must pass on to the recipients the same
+<a id="l36" href="#l36">36</a> freedoms that you received.  You must make sure that they, too, receive
+<a id="l37" href="#l37">37</a> or can get the source code.  And you must show them these terms so they
+<a id="l38" href="#l38">38</a> know their rights.
+<a id="l39" href="#l39">39</a> 
+<a id="l40" href="#l40">40</a>   Developers that use the GNU GPL protect your rights with two steps:
+<a id="l41" href="#l41">41</a> (1) assert copyright on the software, and (2) offer you this License
+<a id="l42" href="#l42">42</a> giving you legal permission to copy, distribute and/or modify it.
+<a id="l43" href="#l43">43</a> 
+<a id="l44" href="#l44">44</a>   For the developers&#x27; and authors&#x27; protection, the GPL clearly explains
+<a id="l45" href="#l45">45</a> that there is no warranty for this free software.  For both users&#x27; and
+<a id="l46" href="#l46">46</a> authors&#x27; sake, the GPL requires that modified versions be marked as
+<a id="l47" href="#l47">47</a> changed, so that their problems will not be attributed erroneously to
+<a id="l48" href="#l48">48</a> authors of previous versions.
+<a id="l49" href="#l49">49</a> 
+<a id="l50" href="#l50">50</a>   Some devices are designed to deny users access to install or run
+<a id="l51" href="#l51">51</a> modified versions of the software inside them, although the manufacturer
+<a id="l52" href="#l52">52</a> can do so.  This is fundamentally incompatible with the aim of
+<a id="l53" href="#l53">53</a> protecting users&#x27; freedom to change the software.  The systematic
+<a id="l54" href="#l54">54</a> pattern of such abuse occurs in the area of products for individuals to
+<a id="l55" href="#l55">55</a> use, which is precisely where it is most unacceptable.  Therefore, we
+<a id="l56" href="#l56">56</a> have designed this version of the GPL to prohibit the practice for those
+<a id="l57" href="#l57">57</a> products.  If such problems arise substantially in other domains, we
+<a id="l58" href="#l58">58</a> stand ready to extend this provision to those domains in future versions
+<a id="l59" href="#l59">59</a> of the GPL, as needed to protect the freedom of users.
+<a id="l60" href="#l60">60</a> 
+<a id="l61" href="#l61">61</a>   Finally, every program is threatened constantly by software patents.
+<a id="l62" href="#l62">62</a> States should not allow patents to restrict development and use of
+<a id="l63" href="#l63">63</a> software on general-purpose computers, but in those that do, we wish to
+<a id="l64" href="#l64">64</a> avoid the special danger that patents applied to a free program could
+<a id="l65" href="#l65">65</a> make it effectively proprietary.  To prevent this, the GPL assures that
+<a id="l66" href="#l66">66</a> patents cannot be used to render the program non-free.
+<a id="l67" href="#l67">67</a> 
+<a id="l68" href="#l68">68</a>   The precise terms and conditions for copying, distribution and
+<a id="l69" href="#l69">69</a> modification follow.

+<a id="l84" href="#l84">84</a>   To &quot;modify&quot; a work means to copy from or adapt all or part of the work
+<a id="l85" href="#l85">85</a> in a fashion requiring copyright permission, other than the making of an
+<a id="l86" href="#l86">86</a> exact copy.  The resulting work is called a &quot;modified version&quot; of the
+<a id="l87" href="#l87">87</a> earlier work or a work &quot;based on&quot; the earlier work.

+<a id="l89" href="#l89">89</a>   A &quot;covered work&quot; means either the unmodified Program or a work based
+<a id="l90" href="#l90">90</a> on the Program.

+<a id="l93" href="#l93">93</a> permission, would make you directly or secondarily liable for
+<a id="l94" href="#l94">94</a> infringement under applicable copyright law, except executing it on a
+<a id="l95" href="#l95">95</a> computer or modifying a private copy.  Propagation includes copying,
+<a id="l96" href="#l96">96</a> distribution (with or without modification), making available to the
+<a id="l97" href="#l97">97</a> public, and in some countries other activities as well.
+<a id="l98" href="#l98">98</a> 
+<a id="l99" href="#l99">99</a>   To &quot;convey&quot; a work means any kind of propagation that enables other
+<a id="l100" href="#l100">100</a> parties to make or receive copies.  Mere interaction with a user through
+<a id="l101" href="#l101">101</a> a computer network, with no transfer of a copy, is not conveying.

+<a id="l103" href="#l103">103</a>   An interactive user interface displays &quot;Appropriate Legal Notices&quot;
+<a id="l104" href="#l104">104</a> to the extent that it includes a convenient and prominently visible

+<a id="l108" href="#l108">108</a> work under this License, and how to view a copy of this License.  If
+<a id="l109" href="#l109">109</a> the interface presents a list of user commands or options, such as a
+<a id="l110" href="#l110">110</a> menu, a prominent item in the list meets this criterion.

+<a id="l113" href="#l113">113</a>

+<a id="l115" href="#l115">115</a> for making modifications to it.  &quot;Object code&quot; means any non-source
+<a id="l116" href="#l116">116</a> form of a work.

+<a id="l118" href="#l118">118</a>   A &quot;Standard Interface&quot; means an interface that either is an official
+<a id="l119" href="#l119">119</a> standard defined by a recognized standards body, or, in the case of
+<a id="l120" href="#l120">120</a> interfaces specified for a particular programming language, one that
+<a id="l121" href="#l121">121</a> is widely used among developers working in that language.

+<a id="l123" href="#l123">123</a>   The &quot;System Libraries&quot; of an executable work include anything, other
+<a id="l124" href="#l124">124</a> than the work as a whole, that (a) is included in the normal form of
+<a id="l125" href="#l125">125</a> packaging a Major Component, but which is not part of that Major
+<a id="l126" href="#l126">126</a> Component, and (b) serves only to enable use of the work with that
+<a id="l127" href="#l127">127</a> Major Component, or to implement a Standard Interface for which an
+<a id="l128" href="#l128">128</a> implementation is available to the public in source code form.  A
+<a id="l129" href="#l129">129</a> &quot;Major Component&quot;, in this context, means a major essential component
+<a id="l130" href="#l130">130</a> (kernel, window system, and so on) of the specific operating system
+<a id="l131" href="#l131">131</a> (if any) on which the executable work runs, or a compiler used to
+<a id="l132" href="#l132">132</a> produce the work, or an object code interpreter used to run it.
+<a id="l133" href="#l133">133</a> 
+<a id="l134" href="#l134">134</a>   The &quot;Corresponding Source&quot; for a work in object code form means all
+<a id="l135" href="#l135">135</a> the source code needed to generate, install, and (for an executable
+<a id="l136" href="#l136">136</a> work) run the object code and to modify the work, including scripts to

+<a id="l140" href="#l140">140</a> which are not part of the work.  For example, Corresponding Source
+<a id="l141" href="#l141">141</a> includes interface definition files associated with source files for
+<a id="l142" href="#l142">142</a> the work, and the source code for shared libraries and dynamically
+<a id="l143" href="#l143">143</a> linked subprograms that the work is specifically designed to require,
+<a id="l144" href="#l144">144</a> such as by intimate data communication or control flow between those
+<a id="l145" href="#l145">145</a> subprograms and other parts of the work.

+<a id="l147" href="#l147">147</a>   The Corresponding Source need not include anything that users
+<a id="l148" href="#l148">148</a> can regenerate automatically from other parts of the Corresponding
+<a id="l149" href="#l149">149</a> Source.

+<a id="l151" href="#l151">151</a>   The Corresponding Source for a work in source code form is that

+<a id="l289" href="#l289">289</a>     you inform other peers where the object code and Corresponding
+<a id="l290" href="#l290">290</a>     Source of the work are being offered to the general public at no
+<a id="l291" href="#l291">291</a>     charge under subsection 6d.

+<a id="l294" href="#l294">294</a> from the Corresponding Source as a System Library, need not be
+<a id="l295" href="#l295">295</a> included in conveying the object code work.

+<a id="l297" href="#l297">297</a>   A &quot;User Product&quot; is either (1) a &quot;consumer product&quot;, which means any
+<a id="l298" href="#l298">298</a> tangible personal property which is normally used for personal, family,
+<a id="l299" href="#l299">299</a> or household purposes, or (2) anything designed or sold for incorporation
+<a id="l300" href="#l300">300</a> into a dwelling.  In determining whether a product is a consumer product,
+<a id="l301" href="#l301">301</a> doubtful cases shall be resolved in favor of coverage.  For a particular
+<a id="l302" href="#l302">302</a> product received by a particular user, &quot;normally used&quot; refers to a
+<a id="l303" href="#l303">303</a> typical or common use of that class of product, regardless of the status
+<a id="l304" href="#l304">304</a> of the particular user or of the way in which the particular user
+<a id="l305" href="#l305">305</a> actually uses, or expects or is expected to use, the product.  A product
+<a id="l306" href="#l306">306</a> is a consumer product regardless of whether the product has substantial
+<a id="l307" href="#l307">307</a> commercial, industrial or non-consumer uses, unless such uses represent
+<a id="l308" href="#l308">308</a> the only significant mode of use of the product.

+<a id="l312" href="#l312">312</a> and execute modified versions of a covered work in that User Product from

+<a id="l315" href="#l315">315</a> code is in no case prevented or interfered with solely because
+<a id="l316" href="#l316">316</a> modification has been made.

+<span class="a">+            (wd . windmove-down)</span>
+<span class="a">+            (wu . windmove-up)</span>
+<span class="a">+            (del . delete-other-windows)</span>
+<span class="a">+            (cf . make-frame-command)</span>
+<span class="a">+            (nf . other-frame))</span>
+      (cf del r r wr wr r d wr d wl wl d wl d wr n 
+          wr n wr n wl wl wl wd n wr n wr n wr n wl wl wl wu nf)))
+ </pre>
+</div></body></html>

+<h1>Forthe</h1><span class="desc">Forth-like DSL.</span><p class="url">git clone https://github.com/equwal/Forthe</p><p><a href="../../Forthe/index.html">Log</a> | <a href="../../Forthe/files.html">Files</a> | <a href="../../Forthe/refs.html">Refs</a></p><hr/>

+ ** Compiles to Elisp.
+ Elisp is hated by many, and for good reason. It is an outdated and clunky lisp.
+ Many have tried and failed to port Emacs to Common Lisp, without success. Forth,
+diff --git a/eforth.el b/eforth.el
+index 67a55b7..9eeb74b 100644
+--- a/eforth.el
++++ b/eforth.el
+<span class="h">@@ -1,43 +1,165 @@</span>
+<span class="d">-(defun add-to-each (it list-of-lists)</span>
+<span class="d">-  (let ((l))</span>
+<span class="d">-    (dolist (i list-of-lists (nreverse l))</span>
+<span class="d">-      (push (list it i) l))))</span>
+<span class="d">-(defun inner-eforth (lang list)</span>
+<span class="d">-  &quot;Forth without a return stack.&quot;</span>
+<span class="a">+;;; eforth.el --- FORTH in elisp. -*- lexical-binding: t; -*-</span>
+<span class="a">+;; Copyright (C) 2019 Spenser Truex</span>
+<span class="a">+;; Author: Spenser Truex &lt;web@spensertruex.com&gt;</span>
+<span class="a">+;; Created: 2019-06-22</span>
+<span class="a">+;; Version: 0.0.1</span>
+<span class="a">+;; Package-Requires: ((emacs &quot;24&quot;))</span>
+<span class="a">+;; Keywords:</span>
+<span class="a">+;; URL: https://</span>
+<span class="a">+;; Homepage:</span>
+<span class="a">+;; This file is not part of GNU Emacs.</span>
+<span class="a">+;; This file is free software.</span>
+<span class="a">+;; License:</span>
+<span class="a">+;; Licensed with the GNU GPL v3 see:</span>
+<span class="a">+;; &lt;https://www.gnu.org/licenses/&gt;.</span>
+<span class="a">+;;; Commentary:</span>
+<span class="a">+;;</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+This is an implementation of an elisp-based FORTH. This means that elisp</span>
+<span class="a">+is used to write the function definitions. In fact, no FORTH function</span>
+<span class="a">+definitions are provided at all to the eForth macro from the library.</span>
+<span class="a">+The main benefit comes from reducing the verbosity of elisp in a small</span>
+<span class="a">+Domain Specific Language the programmer defines.</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+    (require &#x27;eforth)</span>
+<span class="a">+    (defun 8pan ()</span>
+<span class="a">+      (interactive)</span>
+<span class="a">+      (eforth &#x27;((n . (lambda () (switch-to-buffer nil)))</span>
+<span class="a">+                (d . split-window-below)</span>
+<span class="a">+                (r . split-window-right)</span>
+<span class="a">+                (wr . windmove-right)</span>
+<span class="a">+                (wl . windmove-left)</span>
+<span class="a">+                (wd . windmove-down)</span>
+<span class="a">+                (wu . windmove-up)</span>
+<span class="a">+                (del . delete-other-windows)</span>
+<span class="a">+                (cf . make-frame-command)</span>
+<span class="a">+                (nf . other-frame))</span>
+<span class="a">+              (cf del r r wr wr r d wr d wl wl d wl d wr n</span>
+<span class="a">+                  wr n wr n wl wl wl wd n wr n wr n wr n wl wl wl wu nf)))</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+HOW DOES A FORTH WORK?</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+Forth code is gone through by the pointer moving through the call stack.</span>
+<span class="a">+When a non-function is found, it is added to the argument stack. When a</span>
+<span class="a">+function is found, it uses up the argument stack if it has arguments, or</span>
+<span class="a">+just runs if it is nullary. Nothing else is provided by the</span>
+<span class="a">+implementation.</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+WHY?</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+Needing to simulate a long set of keypresses to create an eight panel</span>
+<span class="a">+frame (37 in total), the best option seemeed to be obviously to create a</span>
+<span class="a">+miniature language with 1 or 2 letter abbreviations for each. Any</span>
+<span class="a">+arguments to a function can be passed before it in the list, and there</span>
+<span class="a">+is no need to define a return stack.</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+USAGE: EXAMPLE:</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+    (require &#x27;eforth)</span>
+<span class="a">+    (defun 8pan ()</span>
+<span class="a">+      (interactive)</span>
+<span class="a">+      (eforth &#x27;((n . (lambda () (switch-to-buffer nil)))</span>
+<span class="a">+                (d . split-window-below)</span>
+<span class="a">+                (r . split-window-right)</span>
+<span class="a">+                (wr . windmove-right)</span>
+<span class="a">+                (wl . windmove-left)</span>
+<span class="a">+                (wd . windmove-down)</span>
+<span class="a">+                (wu . windmove-up)</span>
+<span class="a">+                (del . delete-other-windows)</span>
+<span class="a">+                (cf . make-frame-command)</span>
+<span class="a">+                (nf . other-frame))</span>
+<span class="a">+              (cf del r r wr wr r d wr d wl wl d wl d wr n</span>
+<span class="a">+                  wr n wr n wl wl wl wd n wr n wr n wr n wl wl wl wu nf)))</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+M-x 8pan will produce a new frame with eight panels (emacs-speak:</span>
+<span class="a">+&quot;windows&quot;). Here is how it works:</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+eforth is called with two arguments: the environment, name to function</span>
+<span class="a">+bindings, and the eforth code list. You can see in this example that</span>
+<span class="a">+there are no arguments used, since all of these are nullary functions.</span>
+<span class="a">+Here is how you might use arguments:</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+    (eforth &#x27;((ff . find-file)) (&quot;~/some-file&quot; ff))</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+Here we ran (find-file &quot;~/some-file&quot;). If you need to reuse a forth,</span>
+<span class="a">+just define an *env* variable:</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+    (defvar *env* &#x27;((&gt;r . to-return-stack) ...))</span>
+<span class="a">+    (eforth *env* (forth code here ...))</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+BENEFITS</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+Concise code.</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+Elisp&#x27;s convention of names like:</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+    org--insert-structure-template-unique-keys</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+(a real function in the org mode) result in extremely verbose code.</span>
+<span class="a">+Using short, lexical names can reduce code size by a large portion.</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+Compiles to Elisp.</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+Elisp is hated by many, and for good reason. It is an outdated and</span>
+<span class="a">+clunky lisp. Many have tried and failed to port Emacs to Common Lisp,</span>
+<span class="a">+without success. Forth, unlike modern lisps, is designed to be easy to</span>
+<span class="a">+implement. Writing a forth compiler is in fact a trivial exercise.</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+DRAWBACKS</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+Elispers might not want to read your eFORTH code</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+eForth is not stable</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+Isn&#x27;t verbose code self-documenting?</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+NO. Documented code is documented. Using short names with the bindings</span>
+<span class="a">+handily available in the same form is equally explicit as writing them</span>
+<span class="a">+out every time.</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+;;; Code:</span>
+<span class="a">+(require &#x27;cl-lib)</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+(defun eforth--inner (lang list)</span>
+<span class="a">+  &quot;Internal function for the `eforth&#x27; macro.</span>
+<span class="a">+LANG are the bindings for the forth.</span>
+<span class="a">+LIST is full of the forth stack (RPN).&quot;</span>
+   (let ((s))
+     (cl-flet ((fn (fn) (if (functionp fn)
+                            fn
+                          (symbol-function fn))))
+       (dolist (i list)
+         (if (assoc i lang)
+<span class="d">-            (let ((fn (cdr (assoc i lang))))</span>
+<span class="a">+            (let ((fn (cadr (assoc i lang))))</span>
+               (if s
+                   (progn (apply (fn fn) s) (setf s nil))
+                 (funcall (fn fn))))
+           (push i s))))))
+<span class="a">+</span>
+ (defmacro eforth (lang list)
+<span class="d">-  `(inner-eforth ,lang &#x27;,list))</span>
+<span class="d">-;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;</span>
+<span class="d">-;; (defvar *my-env* &#x27;((ff . find-file)))        ;;</span>
+<span class="d">-;; (eforth *my-env* (&quot;~/bogus/&quot; ff))            ;;</span>
+<span class="d">-;; (eforth &#x27;((d . split-window-below))          ;;</span>
+<span class="d">-;;      (d))                                    ;;</span>
+<span class="d">-;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;;</span>
+<span class="d">-(defun 8pan ()</span>
+<span class="d">-  (interactive)</span>
+<span class="d">-  (eforth &#x27;((n . (lambda () (switch-to-buffer nil)))</span>
+<span class="d">-           (d . split-window-below)</span>
+<span class="d">-           (r . split-window-right)</span>
+<span class="d">-           (wr . windmove-right)</span>
+<span class="d">-           (wl . windmove-left)</span>
+<span class="d">-           (wd . windmove-down)</span>
+<span class="d">-           (wu . windmove-up)</span>
+<span class="d">-           (del . delete-other-windows)</span>
+<span class="d">-           (cf . make-frame-command)</span>
+<span class="d">-           (nf . other-frame))</span>
+<span class="d">-          ;; (cf nf del r r wr wr r d wr d wl wl d wl d wr n wr n wr n wl wl wl wd n</span>
+<span class="d">-          ;;     wr n wr n wr n wl wl wl wu nf)</span>
+<span class="d">-          (cf del r r wr wr r d wr d wl wl d wl d</span>
+<span class="d">-               n wr n wr n wr n wd n wl n wl n wl n wu)</span>
+<span class="d">-          ))</span>
+<span class="a">+  &quot;Define and execute a forth language.</span>
+<span class="a">+LANG are the let-bindings for the FORTH&#x27;s operators.</span>
+<span class="a">+LIST is the forth&#x27;s stack.&quot;</span>
+<span class="a">+  `(eforth--inner ,lang &#x27;,list))</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+ (provide &#x27;eforth)
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+;Internal function for the `eforth&#x27; macro.ends here</span></pre>
+</div></body></html>

+<span class="a">+#+TITLE: eForth</span>
+<span class="a">+#+AUTHOR: Spenser Truex</span>
+<span class="a">+#+EMAIL: web@spensertruex.com</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+**** Version 0.0.1</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+This is an implementation of an elisp-based FORTH. This means that elisp</span>
+<span class="a">+is used to write the function definitions. In fact, no FORTH function</span>
+<span class="a">+definitions are provided at all to the eForth macro from the library.</span>
+<span class="a">+The main benefit comes from reducing the verbosity of elisp in a small</span>
+<span class="a">+Domain Specific Language the programmer defines.</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+* How? Forth code is</span>
+<span class="a">+gone through by the pointer moving through the call stack. When a</span>
+<span class="a">+non-function is found, it is added to the argument stack. When a</span>
+<span class="a">+function is found, it uses up the argument stack if it has arguments, or</span>
+<span class="a">+just runs if it is nullary. Nothing else is provided by the</span>
+<span class="a">+implementation. # Why? Needing to simulate a long set of keypresses to</span>
+<span class="a">+create an eight panel frame (37 in total), the best option seemeed to be</span>
+<span class="a">+obviously to create a miniature language with 1 or 2 letter</span>
+<span class="a">+abbreviations for each. Any arguments to a function can be passed before</span>
+<span class="a">+it in the list, and there is no need to define a return stack. # Usage:</span>
+<span class="a">+example:</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+#+BEGIN_SRC emacs-lisp :tangle no</span>
+<span class="a">+(require &#x27;eforth)</span>
+<span class="a">+(defun 8pan ()</span>
+<span class="a">+  (interactive)</span>
+<span class="a">+  (eforth &#x27;((n . (lambda () (switch-to-buffer nil)))</span>
+<span class="a">+            (d . split-window-below)</span>
+<span class="a">+            (r . split-window-right)</span>
+<span class="a">+            (wr . windmove-right)</span>
+<span class="a">+            (wl . windmove-left)</span>
+<span class="a">+            (wd . windmove-down)</span>
+<span class="a">+            (wu . windmove-up)</span>
+<span class="a">+            (del . delete-other-windows)</span>
+<span class="a">+            (cf . make-frame-command)</span>
+<span class="a">+            (nf . other-frame))</span>
+<span class="a">+          (cf del r r wr wr r d wr d wl wl d wl d wr n</span>
+<span class="a">+              wr n wr n wl wl wl wd n wr n wr n wr n wl wl wl wu nf)))</span>
+<span class="a">+#+END_SRC</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+=M-x 8pan= will produce a new frame with eight panels (emacs-speak:</span>
+<span class="a">+&quot;windows&quot;). Here is how it works:</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+=eforth= is called with two arguments: the environment, name to function</span>
+<span class="a">+bindings, and the eforth code list. You can see in this example that</span>
+<span class="a">+there are no arguments used, since all of these are nullary functions.</span>
+<span class="a">+Here is how you might use arguments:</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+#+BEGIN_SRC emacs-lisp :tangle no</span>
+<span class="a">+(eforth &#x27;((ff . find-file)) (&quot;~/some-file&quot; ff))</span>
+<span class="a">+#+END_SRC</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+Here we ran =(find-file &quot;~/some-file&quot;)=. If you need to reuse a forth,</span>
+<span class="a">+just define an =*env*= variable:</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+#+BEGIN_SRC emacs-lisp :tangle no</span>
+<span class="a">+(defvar *env* &#x27;((&gt;r . to-return-stack) ...))</span>
+<span class="a">+(eforth *env* (forth code here ...))</span>
+<span class="a">+#+END_SRC</span>
+<span class="a">+</span>
+<span class="a">+* Benefits</span>
+<span class="a">+** Concise code.</span>
+<span class="a">+Elisp&#x27;s convention of names like:</span>
+<span class="a">+#+BEGIN_SRC emacs-lisp :tangle yes</span>
+<span class="a">+org--insert-structure-template-unique-keys</span>
+<span class="a">+#+END_SRC</span>
